It produces:\n\n- A ranked list of important files\n- A ranked list of important functions and methods\n- A summary of existing unit tests and inferred oracles\n- A final shortlist of functions-under-test (FUTs) for fuzzing, each with a structured \"note to self\" for follow-up actions\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this skill when the user asks to:\n\n- Find the best functions to fuzz in a Python package/library\n- Identify parsers, decoders, validators, or boundary code that is fuzz-worthy\n- Decide what to fuzz based on existing test coverage and API surface\n- Produce a structured shortlist of fuzz targets with harness guidance\n- Automating testing pipeline setup for a new or existing Python project.\n\nDo not use this skill when the user primarily wants to fix a specific bug,\nrefactor code, or implement a harness immediately\n(unless they explicitly ask for target selection first).\n\n## Inputs expected from the environment\n\nThe agent should assume access to:\n\n- Repository filesystem\n- Ability to read files\n- Ability to run local commands (optional but recommended)\n\nPreferred repository listing command:\n\n- Use `tree` to get a fast, high-signal view of repository structure (limit depth if needed).\n\nIf `tree` is not available, fall back to a recursive listing via other standard shell tooling.\n\n## Outputs\n\nProduce result:\n\nA report in any format with the following information: - Localize important files - Localize important functions - Summarize existing unit tests - Decide function under test for fuzzing\nThis file should be placed in the root of the repository as `APIs.txt`,\nwhich servers as the guidelines for future fuzzing harness implementation.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Prefer analysis and reporting over code changes.\n- Do not modify source code unless the user explicitly requests changes.\n- If running commands could be disruptive, default to read-only analysis.\n- Avoid assumptions about runtime behavior; base conclusions on code and tests.\n- Explicitly consider existing tests in the repo when selecting targets and deciding harness shape.\n- Keep the final FUT shortlist small (typically 1–5).\n\n---\n\n# Localize important files\n\n## Goal\n\nProduce a ranked list of files that are most likely to contain fuzz-worthy logic: parsing, decoding, deserialization, validation, protocol handling, file/network boundaries, or native bindings.\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Build a repository map\n    - Start with a repository overview using `tree` to identify:\n        - Root packages and layouts (src/ layout vs flat layout)\n        - Test directories and configs\n        - Bindings/native directories\n        - Examples, docs, and tooling directories\n    - Identify packaging and metadata files such as:\n        - pyproject.toml\n        - setup.cfg\n        - setup.py\n    - Identify test configuration and entry points:\n        - tests/\n        - conftest.py\n        - pytest.ini\n        - tox.ini\n        - noxfile.py\n\n2. Exclude low-value areas\n    - Skip: virtual environments, build outputs, vendored code, documentation-only directories, examples-only directories, generated files.\n\n3. Score files using explainable heuristics\n   Assign a file a higher score when it matches more of these indicators:\n    - Public API exposure: **init**.py re-exports, **all**, api modules\n    - Input boundary keywords in file path or symbols: parse, load, dump, decode, encode, deserialize, serialize, validate, normalize, schema, protocol, message\n    - Format handlers: json, yaml, xml, csv, toml, protobuf, msgpack, pickle\n    - Regex-heavy or templating-heavy code\n    - Native boundaries: ctypes, cffi, cython, extension modules, bindings\n    - Central modules: high import fan-in across the package\n    - Test adjacency: directly imported or heavily referenced by tests\n\n4. Rank and select\n    - Produce a Top-N list (default 10–30) with a short rationale per file.\n\n## Output format\n\nFor each file:\n\n- path\n- score (relative, not necessarily normalized)\n- rationale (2–5 bullets)\n- indicators_hit (list)\n\n---\n\n# Localize important functions\n\n## Goal\n\nFrom the important files, identify and rank functions or methods that are strong fuzz targets while minimizing full-body reading until needed.\n\n## Approach 1: AST-based header and docstring scan\n\nFor each localized Python file, parse it using Python’s `ast` module and extract only:\n\n- Module docstring\n- Function and async function headers (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class headers and method headers\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nDo not read full function bodies during the initial pass unless needed for disambiguation or final selection.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Build per-file declarations via AST\n    - Parse the file with `ast`\n    - Enumerate:\n        - Top-level functions\n        - Classes and their methods\n        - Nested functions only if they are likely to be directly fuzzable via an exposed wrapper\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Signature details (as available from AST)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Location information (file, line range if available)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using headers and docstrings\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Read important files sequentially\n    - For each file from the important files list, read the full contents.\n    - Extract by direct inspection:\n        - Top-level functions and their complete implementations\n        - Classes and their methods with full bodies\n        - Nested functions if they are exposed or called by public APIs\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Complete signature (from source text)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Full function body\n        - Location information (file, approximate line range)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using full source analysis\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Contain complex control flow, loops, or recursive calls\n    - Handle exceptions or edge cases\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Analyze implementation details from full bodies\n   For each candidate function, inspect the body for:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions (explicit checks, assertions, early returns)\n    - Internal helpers called and their purposes\n    - Error handling style and exception types raised\n    - Invariants and postconditions (explicit or implicit)\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n    - Input transformations and data flow\n    - Native calls or external process invocations\n    - Resource allocation and cleanup patterns\n\n4. Use tests to refine candidate selection\n    - Check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n    - Note which aspects of each function are tested vs untested.\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from full body analysis)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n    - Observable implementation risks (unsafe operations, unchecked inputs, complex state)\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n- implementation_notes (key observations from body analysis)\n\n---\n\n# Summarize existing unit tests\n\n## Goal\n\nSummarize what is already tested, infer test oracles, and identify gaps that fuzzing can complement.\n\n## Hard requirement\n\nAlways inspect and incorporate existing tests in the repository when:\n\n- Ranking functions\n- Selecting FUTs\n- Designing input models and oracles\n- Proposing seed corpus sources\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Inventory tests\n    - Locate tests and their discovery configuration (pytest.ini, pyproject.toml, tox.ini, noxfile.py).\n    - Enumerate test modules and map them to source modules via imports.\n    - Identify shared fixtures and data factories (conftest.py, fixture files, test utilities).\n\n2. Summarize test intent\n   For each test module:\n    - What behaviors are asserted\n    - What inputs are used\n    - What exceptions are expected\n    - What invariants are implied\n\n3. Infer oracles and properties\n   Common fuzz-friendly oracles include:\n    - Round-trip properties\n    - Idempotence of normalization\n    - Parser consistency across equivalent inputs\n    - Deterministic output given deterministic input\n    - No-crash and no-hang for malformed inputs\n\n4. Identify coverage gaps\n    - FUT candidates with no direct tests\n    - Input classes not covered by tests (size extremes, malformed encodings, deep nesting, edge unicode, invalid schemas)\n    - Code paths guarded by complex conditionals or exception handlers with no tests\n\n## Output format\n\n- test_map: module_under_test → tests → asserted behaviors\n- inferred_oracles: list of reusable invariants with the functions they apply to\n- gaps: ranked list of untested or weakly-tested candidates\n\n---\n\n# Decide function under test for fuzzing\n\n## Goal\n\nSelect a final set of FUTs (typically 1–5) and produce a structured \"note to self\" for each FUT so the agent can proceed to harness implementation, corpus seeding, and fuzz execution.\n\n## Selection criteria\n\nPrefer FUTs that maximize:\n\n- Security/robustness payoff (parsing, decoding, validation, native boundary)\n- Reachability with minimal setup\n- Low existing test assurance or narrow test input coverage\n- High fuzzability (simple input channel, clear oracle or crash-only target)\n\nExplicitly weigh:\n\n- What tests already cover (and what they do not)\n- What seeds can be extracted from tests, fixtures, and sample data\n\n## Required \"note to self\" template\n\nFor each selected FUT, produce exactly this structure:\n\nFuzzing Target Note\n\n- Target:\n- File / location:\n- Why this target:\n    - Input surface:\n    - Boundary or native considerations:\n    - Complexity or path depth:\n    - Current test gaps:\n- Callable contract:\n    - Required imports or initialization:\n    - Preconditions:\n    - Determinism concerns:\n    - External dependencies:\n- Input model:\n    - Primary payload type:\n    - Decoding or parsing steps:\n    - Constraints to respect:\n    - Edge classes to emphasize:\n- Oracles:\n    - Must-hold properties:\n    - Acceptable exceptions:\n    - Suspicious exceptions:\n    - Crash-only vs correctness-checking:\n- Harness plan:\n    - Recommended approach:\n    - Minimal harness signature:\n    - Seed corpus ideas:\n    - Timeouts and resource limits:\n- Risk flags:\n    - Native extension involved:\n    - Potential DoS paths:\n    - External I/O:\n- Next actions:\n    1.\n    2.\n    3.\n    4.\n\n## Output format\n\n- selected_futs: list of chosen FUTs with brief justification\n- notes_to_self: one \"Fuzzing Target Note\" per FUT\n\n---\n\n# Final JSON block\n\nAt the end of the report, include a JSON object with:\n\n- important_files: [{path, score, rationale, indicators_hit}]\n- important_functions: [{qualname, file, line_range, score, rationale, dependencies, harnessability}]\n- test_summary: {test_map, inferred_oracles, gaps}\n- selected_futs: [{qualname, file, line_range, justification}]\n- notes_to_self: [{target_qualname, note}]\n\n\nARGUMENTS: /app/arrow"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c4870ea9-83dc-4fcc-99e6-806ec7aea3b8","uuid":"dec5b2ea-d35a-4569-9e09-42eee301c8ad","isSynthetic":true}

It implements and updates the datetime type, plugging gaps in functionality and providing an intelligent module API that supports many common creation scenarios. Simply put, it helps you work with dates and times with fewer imports and a lot less code.\n\nArrow is named after the `arrow of time <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrow_of_time>`_ and is heavily inspired by `moment.js <https://github.com/moment/moment>`_ and `requests <https://github.com/psf/requests>`_.\n\nWhy use Arrow over built-in modules?\n------------------------------------\n\nPython's standard library and some other low-level modules have near-complete date, time and timezone functionality, but don't work very well from a usability perspective:\n\n- Too many modules: datetime, time, calendar, dateutil, pytz and more\n- Too many types: date, time, datetime, tzinfo, timedelta, relativedelta, etc.\n- Timezones and timestamp conversions are verbose and unpleasant\n- Timezone naivety is the norm\n- Gaps in functionality: ISO 8601 parsing, timespans, humanization\n\nFeatures\n--------\n\n- Fully-implemented, drop-in replacement for datetime\n- Support for Python 3.8+\n- Timezone-aware and UTC by default\n- Super-simple creation options for many common input scenarios\n- ``shift`` method with support for relative offsets, including weeks\n- Format and parse strings automatically\n- Wide support for the `ISO 8601 <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_8601>`_ standard\n- Timezone conversion\n- Support for ``dateutil``, ``pytz``, and ``ZoneInfo`` tzinfo objects\n- Generates time spans, ranges, floors and ceilings for time frames ranging from microsecond to year\n- Humanize dates and times with a growing list of contributed locales\n- Extensible for your own Arrow-derived types\n- Full support for PEP 484-style type hints\n\nQuick Start\n-----------\n\nInstallation\n~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\nTo install Arrow, use `pip <https://pip.pypa.io/en/stable/quickstart/>`_ or `pipenv <https://docs.pipenv.org>`_:\n\n.. code-block:: console\n\n    $ pip install -U arrow\n\nExample Usage\n~~~~~~~~~~~~~\n\n.. code-block:: python\n\n    >>> import arrow\n    >>> arrow.get('2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00')\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+07:00]>\n\n    >>> utc = arrow.utcnow()\n    >>> utc\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T21:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n\n    >>> utc = utc.shift(hours=-1)\n    >>> utc\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T20:23:58.970460+00:00]>\n\n    >>> local = utc.to('US/Pacific')\n    >>> local\n    <Arrow [2013-05-11T13:23:58.970460-07:00]>\n\n    >>> local.timestamp()\n    1368303838.970460\n\n    >>> local.format()\n    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n\n    >>> local.format('YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss ZZ')\n    '2013-05-11 13:23:58 -07:00'\n\n    >>> local.humanize()\n    'an hour ago'\n\n    >>> local.humanize(locale='ko-kr')\n    '한시간 전'\n\n.. end-inclusion-marker-do-not-remove\n\nDocumentation\n-------------\n\nFor full documentation, please visit `arrow.readthedocs.io <https://arrow.readthedocs.io>`_.\n\nContributing\n------------\n\nContributions are welcome for both code and localizations (adding and updating locales). 283→            )\n   284→\n   285→        has_time = has_space_divider or has_t_divider\n   286→        has_tz = False\n   287→\n   288→        # date formats (ISO 8601 and others) to test against\n   289→        # NOTE: YYYYMM is omitted to avoid confusion with YYMMDD (no longer part of ISO 8601, but is still often used)\n   290→        formats = [\n   291→            \"YYYY-MM-DD\",\n   292→            \"YYYY-M-DD\",\n   293→            \"YYYY-M-D\",\n   294→            \"YYYY/MM/DD\",\n   295→            \"YYYY/M/DD\",\n   296→            \"YYYY/M/D\",\n   297→            \"YYYY.MM.DD\",\n   298→            \"YYYY.M.DD\",\n   299→            \"YYYY.M.D\",\n   300→            \"YYYYMMDD\",\n   301→            \"YYYY-DDDD\",\n   302→            \"YYYYDDDD\",\n   303→ ParserError(\n   324→                    \"Invalid time component provided. \"\n   325→                    \"Please specify a format or provide a valid time component in the basic or extended ISO 8601 time format.\"\n   326→                )\n   327→\n   328→            (\n   329→                hours,\n   330→                minutes,\n   331→                seconds,\n   332→                subseconds_sep,\n   333→                subseconds,\n   334→            ) = time_components.groups()\n   335→\n   336→            has_tz = len(time_parts) == 2\n   337→            has_minutes = minutes is not None\n   338→            has_seconds = seconds is not None\n   339→            has_subseconds = subseconds is not None\n   340→\n   341→            is_basic_time_format = \":\" not in 371→        return self._parse_multiformat(datetime_string, formats)\n   372→\n   373→    def parse(\n   374→        self,\n   375→        datetime_string: str,\n   376→        fmt: Union[List[str], str],\n   377→        normalize_whitespace: bool = False,\n   378→    ) -> datetime:\n   379→        \"\"\"\n   380→        Parses a datetime string using a specified format.\n   381→\n   382→        :param datetime_string: The datetime string to parse.\n   383→        :param fmt: The format string or list of format strings to use for parsing.\n   384→        :param normalize_whitespace: Whether to normalize whitespace in the datetime string (default is False).\n   385→        :type datetime_string: str\n   386→        :type fmt: Union[List[str], str]\n   387→        :type normalize_whitespace: bool\n   388→        :returns: The parsed datetime object.\n   389→        :rtype: datetime\n   390→        :raises ParserMatchError: If the datetime string does not match the specified format.\n   391→\n   392→        Usage::\n   393→\n   394→        >>> import arrow.parser\n   395→        >>> arrow.parser.DateTimeParser().parse('2021-10-12 14:30:00', 'YYYY-MM-DD HH:mm:ss')\n   396→        datetime.datetime(2021, 10, 12, 14, 30)\n   397→\n   398→\n   399→        \"\"\"\n   400→        if normalize_whitespace:\n   401→            datetime_string = re.sub(r\"\\s+\", \" \", datetime_string)\n   402→\n   403→        if isinstance(fmt, list):\n   404→            return self._parse_multiformat(datetime_string, fmt)\n   405→\n   406→        try:\n   407→            fmt_tokens: List[_FORMAT_TYPE]\n   408→            fmt_pattern_re: Pattern[str]\n   409→            fmt_tokens, fmt_pattern_re = self._generate_pattern_re(fmt)\n   410→        except re.error as e:\n   411→            raise ParserMatchError(\n   412→                f\"Failed to generate regular expression pattern: {e}.\"\n   413→            )\n   414→\n   415→        match = fmt_pattern_re.search(datetime_string)\n   416→\n   417→        if match is None:\n   418→            raise ParserMatchError(\n   419→                f\"Failed to match {fmt!r} when parsing {datetime_string!r}.\"\n   420→            )\n   421→\n   422→        parts: _Parts = {}\n   423→        for token in fmt_tokens:\n   424→            value: Union[Tuple[str, str, str], str]\n   425→ value, parts)  # type: ignore[arg-type]\n   438→\n   439→        return self._build_datetime(parts)\n   440→\n   441→    def _generate_pattern_re(self, fmt: str) -> Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]:\n   442→        \"\"\"\n   443→        Generates a regular expression pattern from a format string.\n   444→\n   445→        :param fmt: The format string to convert into a regular expression pattern.\n   446→        :type fmt: str\n   447→        :returns: A tuple containing a list of format tokens and the corresponding regular expression pattern.\n   448→        :rtype: Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]\n   449→        :raises ParserError: If an unrecognized token is encountered in the format string.\n   450→        \"\"\"\n   451→        # fmt is a string of tokens like 'YYYY-MM-DD'\n   452→        # we construct a new string by replacing each\n   453→        # token by its pattern:\n   454→        # 'YYYY-MM-DD' -> '(?P<YYYY>\\d{4})-(?P<MM>\\d{2})-(?P<DD>\\d{2})'\n   455→        tokens: List[_FORMAT_TYPE] = []\n   456→        offset = 0\n   457→\n   458→        # Escape all special RegEx chars\n   459→        escaped_fmt = re.escape(fmt)\n   460→\n   461→        # Extract the bracketed expressions to be reinserted later.\n   462→        escaped_fmt = re.sub(self._ESCAPE_RE, \"#\", escaped_fmt)\n   463→\n   464→        # Any number of S is the same as one.\n   465→        # TODO: allow users to specify the number of digits to parse\n   466→        escaped_fmt = re.sub(r\"S+\", \"S\", escaped_fmt)\n   467→\n   468→        escaped_data = re.findall(self._ESCAPE_RE, fmt)\n   469→\n   470→        fmt_pattern = escaped_fmt\n   471→\n   472→        for m in self._FORMAT_RE.finditer(escaped_fmt):\n   473→            token: _FORMAT_TYPE = cast(_FORMAT_TYPE, m.group(0))\n   474→            try:\n   475→                input_re = self._input_re_map[token]\n   476→            except KeyError:\n   477→                raise ParserError(f\"Unrecognized token {token!r}.\")\n   478→            input_pattern = f\"(?P<{token}>{input_re.pattern})\"\n   479→            tokens.append(token)\n   480→            # a pattern doesn't have the same length as the token\n   481→            # it replaces! We keep the difference in the offset variable.\n   482→            # This works because the string is scanned left-to-right and matches\n   483→            # are returned in the order found by finditer.\n   484→            fmt_pattern = (\n   485→                fmt_pattern[: m.start() + offset]\n   486→                + input_pattern\n   487→                + fmt_pattern[m.end() + offset :]\n   488→            )\n   489→            offset += len(input_pattern) - (m.end() - m.start())\n   490→\n   491→        final_fmt_pattern = \"\"\n   492→        split_fmt = fmt_pattern.split(r\"\\#\")\n   493→\n   494→        # Due to the way Python splits, 'split_fmt' will always be longer\n   495→        for i in range(len(split_fmt)):\n   496→            final_fmt_pattern += split_fmt[i]\n   497→            if i < len(escaped_data):\n   498→                final_fmt_pattern += escaped_data[i][1:-1]\n   499→\n   500→        # Wrap final_fmt_pattern in a custom word boundary to strictly\n   501→        # match the formatting pattern and filter out date and time formats\n   502→        # that include junk such as: blah1998-09-12 blah, blah 1998-09-12blah,\n   503→        # blah1998-09-12blah. The custom word boundary matches every character\n   504→        # that is not a whitespace character to allow for searching for a date\n   505→        # and time string in a natural language sentence. Therefore, searching\n   506→        # for a string of the form YYYY-MM-DD in \"blah 1998-09-12 blah\" will\n   507→        # work properly.\n   508→        # Certain punctuation before or after the target pattern such as\n   509→        # \"1998-09-12,\" is permitted. For the full list of valid punctuation,\n   510→        # see the documentation.\n   511→\n   512→        starting_word_boundary = (\n   513→            r\"(?<!\\S\\S)\"  # Don't have two consecutive non-whitespace characters. This ensures that we allow cases\n   514→            # like .11.25.2019 but not 1.11.25.2019 (for pattern MM.DD.YYYY)\n   515→            r\"(?<![^\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)<>\\s])\"  # This is the list of punctuation that is ok before the\n   516→            # pattern (i.e. \"It can't not be these characters before the pattern\")\n   517→            r\"(\\b|^)\"\n   518→            # The \\b is to block cases like 1201912 but allow 201912 for pattern YYYYMM. The ^ was necessary to allow a\n   519→            # negative number through i.e. before epoch numbers\n   520→        )\n   521→        ending_word_boundary = (\n   522→            r\"(?=[\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)\\<\\>]?\"  # Positive lookahead stating that these punctuation marks\n   523→            # can appear after the pattern at most 1 time\n   524→            r\"(?!\\S))\"  # Don't allow any non-whitespace character after the punctuation\n   525→        )\n   526→        bounded_fmt_pattern = r\"{}{}{}\".format(\n   527→            starting_word_boundary, final_fmt_pattern, ending_word_boundary\n   528→        )\n   529→\n   530→        return tokens, re.compile(bounded_fmt_pattern, flags=re.IGNORECASE)\n   531→\n   532→    @overload\n   533→    def _parse_token(\n   534→        self,\n   535→        token: Literal[\n   536→            \"YYYY\",\n   537→            \"YY\",\n   538→            \"MM\",\n   539→            \"M\",\n   540→            \"DDDD\",\n   541→            \"DDD\",\n   542→            \"DD\",\n   543→            \"D\",\n   544→            \"Do\",\n   545→            \"HH\",\n   546→            \"hh\",\n   547→            \"h\",\n   548→            \"H\",\n   549→            \"mm\",\n   550→            \"m\",\n   551→            \"ss\",\n   552→            \"s\",\n   553→            \"x\",\n   554→        ],\n   555→        value: Union[str, bytes, SupportsInt, bytearray],\n   556→        parts: _Parts,\n   557→    ) -> None: ...  # pragma: no cover\n   558→\n   559→    @overload\n   560→    def _parse_token(\n   561→        self,\n   562→        token: Literal[\"X\"],\n   563→        value: Union[str, bytes, SupportsFloat, bytearray],\n   564→        parts: _Parts,\n   565→    ) -> None: ...  # datetime_string: str,\n        fmt: Union[List[str], str],\n        normalize_whitespace: bool = False,\n    ) -> datetime:\n        \"\"\"\n        Parses a datetime string using a specified    :returns: The parsed datetime object.\n        :rtype: datetime\n        :raises ParserMatchError: If the datetime string does not match the specified format.\n\n        Usage::\n\n        >>> Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Generates a regular expression pattern from a format string.\n\n        :param fmt: The format string to convert into a regular expression pattern.\n        :type fmt: str\n        :returns: A tuple containing a list of format tokens and the corresponding regular expression pattern.\n        :rtype: Tuple[List[_FORMAT_TYPE], Pattern[str]]\n        :raises ParserError: If an unrecognized token is encountered in the format string.\n        \"\"\"\n        # fmt is a string of tokens like 'YYYY-MM-DD'\n        # we construct a new string by replacing each\n        # token by its pattern:\n        # 'YYYY-MM-DD' -> '(?P<YYYY>\\d{4})-(?P<MM>\\d{2})-(?P<DD>\\d{2})'\n        tokens: List[_FORMAT_TYPE] = []\n        offset = 0\n\n        # Escape all special RegEx chars\n        escaped_fmt = re.escape(fmt)\n\n        # Extract the bracketed expressions to be reinserted later.\n        escaped_fmt = re.sub(self._ESCAPE_RE, \"#\", escaped_fmt)\n\n        # Any number of S is the same as one.\n        # TODO: allow users to specify the number of digits to parse\n        escaped_fmt = re.sub(r\"S+\", \"S\", escaped_fmt)\n\n        escaped_data = re.findall(self._ESCAPE_RE, fmt)\n\n        fmt_pattern = escaped_fmt\n\n        for m in self._FORMAT_RE.finditer(escaped_fmt):\n            token: _FORMAT_TYPE = cast(_FORMAT_TYPE, m.group(0))\n            try:\n                input_re = self._input_re_map[token]\n            except KeyError:\n                raise ParserError(f\"Unrecognized token {token!r}.\")\n            input_pattern = f\"(?P<{token}>{input_re.pattern})\"\n            tokens.append(token)\n            # a pattern doesn't have the same length as the token\n            # it replaces! We keep the difference in the offset variable.\n            # This works because the string is scanned left-to-right and matches\n            # are returned in the order found by finditer.\n            fmt_pattern = (\n                fmt_pattern[: m.start() + offset]\n                + input_pattern\n                + fmt_pattern[m.end() + offset :]\n            )\n            offset += len(input_pattern) - (m.end() - m.start())\n\n        final_fmt_pattern = \"\"\n        split_fmt = fmt_pattern.split(r\"\\#\")\n\n        # Due to the way Python splits, 'split_fmt' will always be longer\n        for i in range(len(split_fmt)):\n            final_fmt_pattern += split_fmt[i]\n            if i < len(escaped_data):\n                final_fmt_pattern += escaped_data[i][1:-1]\n\n        # Wrap final_fmt_pattern in a custom word boundary to strictly\n        # match the formatting pattern and filter out date and time formats\n        # that include junk such as: blah1998-09-12 blah, blah 1998-09-12blah,\n        # blah1998-09-12blah. The custom word boundary matches every character\n        # that is not a whitespace character to allow for searching for a date\n        # and time string in a natural language sentence. Therefore, searching\n        # for a string of the form YYYY-MM-DD in \"blah 1998-09-12 blah\" will\n        # work properly.\n        # Certain punctuation before or after the target pattern such as\n        # \"1998-09-12,\" is permitted. For the full list of valid punctuation,\n        # see the documentation.\n\n        starting_word_boundary = (\n            r\"(?<!\\S\\S)\"  # Don't have two consecutive non-whitespace characters. This ensures that we allow cases\n            # like .11.25.2019 but not 1.11.25.2019 (for pattern MM.DD.YYYY)\n            r\"(?<![^\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)<>\\s])\"  # This is the list of punctuation that is ok before the\n            # pattern (i.e. \"It can't not be these characters before the pattern\")\n            r\"(\\b|^)\"\n            # The \\b is to block cases like 1201912 but allow 201912 for pattern YYYYMM. The ^ was necessary to allow a\n            # negative number through i.e. before epoch numbers\n        )\n        ending_word_boundary = (\n            r\"(?=[\\,\\.\\;\\:\\?\\!\\\"\\'\\`\\[\\]\\{\\}\\(\\)\\<\\>]?\"  # Positive lookahead stating that these punctuation marks\n            # can appear after the pattern at most 1 time\n            r\"(?!\\S))\"  # Don't allow any non-whitespace character after the punctuation\n        )\n        bounded_fmt_pattern = r\"{}{}{}\".format(\n            starting_word_boundary, final_fmt_pattern, ending_word_boundary\n        )\n\n        return tokens, re.compile(bounded_fmt_pattern, flags=re.IGNORECASE)\n\n    @overload\n    def _parse_token(\n        self,\n        token: Literal[\n            \"YYYY\",\n            \"YY\",\n            \"MM\",\n            \"M\",\n            \"DDDD\",\n            \"DDD\",\n            \"DD\",\n            \"D\",\n            \"Do\",\n            \"HH\",\n            \"hh\",\n            \"h\",\n            \"H\",\n            \"mm\",\n            \"m\",\n            \"ss\",\n            \"s\",\n            \"x\",\n        ],\n        value: Union[str, bytes, SupportsInt, bytearray],\n        parts: _Parts,\n    ) -> None: ...  # pragma: no cover\n\n    def _parse_token(\n        self,\n        token: Any,\n        value: Any,\n        parts: _Parts,\n    ) -> None:\n        \"\"\"\n        Parse a token and its value, and update the `_Parts` dictionary with the parsed values.\n\n        The function supports several tokens, including \"YYYY\", \"YY\", \"MMMM\", \"MMM\", \"MM\", \"M\", \"DDDD\", \"DDD\", \"DD\", \"D\", \"Do\", \"dddd\", \"ddd\", \"HH\", \"H\", \"mm\", \"m\", \"ss\", \"s\", \"S\", \"X\", \"x\", \"ZZZ\", \"ZZ\", \"Z\", \"a\", \"A\", and \"W\". Each token is matched and the corresponding value is parsed and added to the `_Parts` dictionary.\n\n        :param token: The token to parse.\n        :type token: Any\n        :param value: The value of the token.\n        :type value: Any\n        :param parts: A dictionary to update with the parsed values.\n        :type parts: _Parts\n        :raises ParserMatchError: If the hour token value is not between 0 and 12 inclusive for tokens \"a\" or \"A\".\n\n        \"\"\"\n        if token == \"YYYY\":\n            parts[\"year\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"YY\":\n            value = int(value)\n            parts[\"year\"] = 1900 + value if value > 68 else 2000 + value\n\n        elif token in [\"MMMM\", \"MMM\"]:\n            # FIXME: month_number() is nullable\n            parts[\"month\"] = self.locale.month_number(value.lower())  # type: ignore[typeddict-item]\n\n        elif token in [\"MM\", \"M\"]:\n            parts[\"month\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"DDDD\", \"DDD\"]:\n            parts[\"day_of_year\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"DD\", \"D\"]:\n            parts[\"day\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"Do\":\n            parts[\"day\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"dddd\":\n            # locale day names are 1-indexed\n            day_of_week = [x.lower() for x in self.locale.day_names].index(\n                value.lower()\n            )\n            parts[\"day_of_week\"] = day_of_week - 1\n\n        elif token == \"ddd\":\n            # locale day abbreviations are 1-indexed\n            day_of_week = [x.lower() for x in self.locale.day_abbreviations].index(\n                value.lower()\n            )\n            parts[\"day_of_week\"] = day_of_week - 1\n\n        elif token.upper() in [\"HH\", \"H\"]:\n            parts[\"hour\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"mm\", \"m\"]:\n            parts[\"minute\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"ss\", \"s\"]:\n            parts[\"second\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token == \"S\":\n            # We have the *most significant* digits of an arbitrary-precision integer.\n            # We want the six most significant digits as an integer, rounded.\n            # IDEA: add nanosecond support somehow? Need datetime support for it first.\n            value = value.ljust(7, \"0\")\n\n            # floating-point (IEEE-754) defaults to half-to-even rounding\n            seventh_digit = int(value[6])\n            if seventh_digit == 5:\n                rounding = int(value[5]) % 2\n            elif seventh_digit > 5:\n                rounding = 1\n            else:\n                rounding = 0\n\n            parts[\"microsecond\"] = int(value[:6]) + rounding\n\n        elif token == \"X\":\n            parts[\"timestamp\"] = float(value)\n\n        elif token == \"x\":\n            parts[\"expanded_timestamp\"] = int(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"ZZZ\", \"ZZ\", \"Z\"]:\n            parts[\"tzinfo\"] = TzinfoParser.parse(value)\n\n        elif token in [\"a\", \"A\"]:\n            if value in (self.locale.meridians[\"am\"], self.locale.meridians[\"AM\"]):\n                parts[\"am_pm\"] = \"am\"\n                if \"hour\" in parts and not 0 <= parts[\"hour\"] <= 12:\n                    raise ParserMatchError(\n                        f\"Hour token value must be between 0 and 12 inclusive for token {token!r}.\"\n                    )\n            elif value in (self.locale.meridians[\"pm\"], self.locale.meridians[\"PM\"]):\n                parts[\"am_pm\"] = \"pm\"\n        elif token == \"W\":\n            parts[\"weekdate\"] = value\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def _build_datetime(parts: _Parts) -> datetime:\n        \"\"\"\n        Build a datetime object from a dictionary of date parts.\n\n        :param parts: A dictionary containing the date parts    if month is not None:\n                raise ParserError(\n                    \"Month component is not allowed with the DDD and DDDD tokens.\"\n                )\n\n            date_string =       day_of_week: Optional[int] = parts.get(\"day_of_week\")\n        day = parts.get(\"day\")\n\n        # If day is passed, ignore day of week\n        if day_of_week is not None and day is None:\n            year = parts.get(\"year\", 1970)\n            month = parts.get(\"month\", 1)\n            day = 1\n\n            # dddd => first day of week after epoch\n            # dddd YYYY => first day of week in specified year\n            # dddd MM YYYY => first day of week in specified year and month\n            # dddd MM => first day after epoch in specified month\n            next_weekday_dt = next_weekday(datetime(year, month, day), day_of_week)\n            parts[\"year\"] = next_weekday_dt.year\n            parts[\"month\"] = next_weekday_dt.month\n datetime:\n        \"\"\"\n        Parse a date and time string using multiple formats.\n\n        Tries to parse the provided string with each format in the given `formats`\n        iterable, returning the resulting `datetime` object if a match is found. If no\n        format matches the string, a `ParserError` is raised.\n\n        :param string: The date and time string to parse.\n        :type string: str\n        :param formats: An iterable of date and time format strings to try, in order.\n        :type formats: Iterable[str]\n        :returns: The parsed date and time.\n        :rtype: datetime.datetime\n        :raises ParserError: If no format matches the input string.\n        \"\"\"\n        _datetime: Optional[datetime] = None\n\n        for fmt in formats:\n            try:\n                _datetime = self.parse(string, fmt)\n                break\n            except ParserMatchError:\n                pass\n\n        if _datetime is None:\n            supported_formats = \", \".join(formats)\n            raise ParserError(\n                f\"Could not match input {string!r} to any of the following formats: {supported_formats}.\"\n            )\n\n        return _datetime\n\n    # generates a capture group of choices separated by an OR operator\n    @staticmethod\n    def _generate_choice_re(\n        choices: Iterable[str], flags: Union[int, re.RegexFlag] = 0\n    ) -> Pattern[str]:\n        \"\"\"\n        Generate a regular expression pattern that matches a choice from an iterable.\n\n        Takes an iterable of strings (`choices`) and returns a compiled regular expression\n        pattern that matches any of the choices. The pattern is created by joining the\n        choices with the '|' (OR) operator, which matches any of the enclosed patterns.\n\n        :param choices: An iterable of strings to match.\n        :type choices: Iterable[str]\n        :param flags: Optional regular expression flags. Default is 0.\n        :type flags: Union[int, re.RegexFlag], optional\n        :returns: A compiled regular expression pattern that matches any of the choices.\n        :rtype: re.Pattern[str]\n        \"\"\"\n        return re.compile(r\"({})\".format(\"|\".join(choices)), flags=flags)\n\n\nclass TzinfoParser:\n    \"\"\"\n    Parser for timezone information.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    _TZINFO_RE: Locale-specific edge cases with unusual month/day names\n9. Timezone strings with unusual formats or invalid names\n10. Microsecond rounding edge cases\n\n================================================================================\n## 4. SELECTED FUNCTIONS UNDER TEST (FUTs)\n================================================================================\n\n### FUT #1: DateTimeParser.parse_iso()\n\n**Why this target:**\n- High-value parsing function accepting untrusted string input\n- Complex logic with multiple code paths\n- Regex-heavy implementation prone to ReDoS\n- Core functionality with security implications\n- Existing tests focus on valid inputs, limited adversarial testing\n\n**Callable contract:**\n- Required imports: from arrow.parser import DateTimeParser\n- Initialization: parser = DateTimeParser()\n- Preconditions: Input must be a string\n- Determinism: Deterministic for same input\n- External dependencies: timezone database (tzdata/zoneinfo)\n\n**Input model:**\n- Primary payload: arbitrary string (datetime_string parameter)\n- Secondary input: boolean (normalize_whitespace parameter)\n- Constraints: None (accepts any string)\n- Edge classes to emphasize:\n  - ISO 8601 variants (basic, extended, with/without timezone)\n  - Malformed ISO-like strings\n  - Extremely long strings\n  - Special characters and unicode\n  - Empty strings and whitespace-only strings\n  - Strings with embedded nulls\n\n**Oracles:**\n- Must-hold: No uncaught exceptions (only ParserError allowed)\n- Acceptable: ParserError for invalid input\n- Suspicious: Any exception other than ParserError, hangs, crashes\n- Strategy: Crash-only fuzzing + exception type checking\n\n**Harness plan:**\n- Approach: LibFuzzer with atheris\n- Minimal harness: fuzz(data) -> parser.parse_iso(data.decode('utf-8', 'ignore'))\n- Seed corpus: Valid ISO 8601 strings from tests\n- Timeouts: 1 second per input\n- Resource limits: Standard\n\n**Risk flags:**\n- Native extension: No (pure Python)\n- Potential DoS: Yes (regex backtracking)\n- External I/O: Yes (timezone database reads)\n\n**Next actions:**\n1. Create atheris-based fuzz harness for parse_iso()\n2. Extract seed corpus from test_parser.py\n3. Run fuzzer for 10 seconds to validate\n4. Monitor for ParserError vs other exceptions\n\n\n### FUT #2: DateTimeParser.parse()\n\n**Why this target:**\n- Accepts two string inputs (datetime + format)\n- Format string controls regex generation\n- High potential for regex injection and malformed patterns\n- More complex than parse_iso due to custom format handling\n\n**Callable contract:**\n- Required imports: from arrow.parser import DateTimeParser\n- Initialization: parser = DateTimeParser()\n- Preconditions: Two string inputs\n- Determinism: Deterministic\n- External dependencies: timezone database\n\n**Input model:**\n- Primary payload: datetime string and format string (two separate inputs)\n- For fuzzing: concatenate with delimiter, split in harness\n- Edge classes:\n  - Valid format tokens: YYYY, MM, DD, HH, mm, ss, etc.\n  - Invalid/malicious format tokens\n  - Extremely long format strings\n  - Format strings with special regex characters\n  - Mismatched datetime/format pairs\n\n**Oracles:**\n- Must-hold: No uncaught exceptions\n- Acceptable: ParserError, ParserMatchError\n- Suspicious: re.error, other exceptions\n\n**Harness plan:**\n- Approach: Structured fuzzing with format string generation\n- Split input into datetime_string and format_string\n- Use delimiter (e.g., ||| separator)\n\n**Risk flags:**\n- Regex compilation: Yes (format string controls regex)\n- Potential DoS: Yes (malicious format patterns)\n- External I/O: Yes (timezone database)\n\n**Next actions:**\n1. Create harness with input splitting\n2. Generate seed corpus with common format patterns\n3. Fuzz both valid and invalid format combinations\n4. Monitor regex compilation errors\n\n\n### FUT #3: TzinfoParser.parse()\n\n**Why this target:**\n- Parses timezone strings from untrusted input\n- Performs ZoneInfo lookups with external data\n- Arithmetic operations on timezone offsets\n- Relatively simple input/output for high-value boundary\n\n**Callable contract:**\n- Required imports: from arrow.parser import TzinfoParser\n- Class method (no initialization needed)\n- Preconditions: String input\n- Determinism: Deterministic\n- External dependencies: zoneinfo database\n\n**Input model:**\n- Primary payload: timezone string\n- Edge classes:\n  - Valid: UTC, Z, +05:30, -08:00, America/New_York\n  - Malformed: +999:999, invalid zone names\n  - Edge: empty string, very long strings\n  - Special: unicode, null bytes\n\n**Oracles:**\n- Must-hold: No uncaught exceptions\n- Acceptable: ParserError\n- Suspicious: ZoneInfoNotFoundError should be caught\n\n**Harness plan:**\n- Simple single-input harness\n- Seed with valid timezone names and offset formats\n\n**Risk flags:**\n- File I/O: Yes (zoneinfo database)\n- Arithmetic: Yes (timezone offset calculations)\n\n**Next actions:**\n1. Create simple harness for timezone parsing\n2. Seed with /usr/share/zoneinfo entries\n3. Test malformed offset strings\n\n\n================================================================================\n## 5. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/discover-important-function\n\n# Fuzz Target Localizer\n\n## Purpose\n\nThis skill helps an agent quickly narrow a Python repository down to a small set of high-value fuzz targets. It produces:\n\n- A ranked list of important files\n- A ranked list of important functions and methods\n- A summary of existing unit tests and inferred oracles\n- A final shortlist of functions-under-test (FUTs) for fuzzing, each with a structured \"note to self\" for follow-up actions\n\n## When to use\n\nUse this skill when the user asks to:\n\n- Find the best functions to fuzz in a Python package/library\n- Identify parsers, decoders, validators, or boundary code that is fuzz-worthy\n- Decide what to fuzz based on existing test coverage and API surface\n- Produce a structured shortlist of fuzz targets with harness guidance\n- Automating testing pipeline setup for a new or existing Python project.\n\nDo not use this skill when the user primarily wants to fix a specific bug,\nrefactor code, or implement a harness immediately\n(unless they explicitly ask for target selection first).\n\n## Inputs expected from the environment\n\nThe agent should assume access to:\n\n- Repository filesystem\n- Ability to read files\n- Ability to run local commands (optional but recommended)\n\nPreferred repository listing command:\n\n- Use `tree` to get a fast, high-signal view of repository structure (limit depth if needed).\n\nIf `tree` is not available, fall back to a recursive listing via other standard shell tooling.\n\n## Outputs\n\nProduce result:\n\nA report in any format with the following information: - Localize important files - Localize important functions - Summarize existing unit tests - Decide function under test for fuzzing\nThis file should be placed in the root of the repository as `APIs.txt`,\nwhich servers as the guidelines for future fuzzing harness implementation.\n\n## Guardrails\n\n- Prefer analysis and reporting over code changes.\n- Do not modify source code unless the user explicitly requests changes.\n- If running commands could be disruptive, default to read-only analysis.\n- Avoid assumptions about runtime behavior; base conclusions on code and tests.\n- Explicitly consider existing tests in the repo when selecting targets and deciding harness shape.\n- Keep the final FUT shortlist small (typically 1–5).\n\n---\n\n# Localize important files\n\n## Goal\n\nProduce a ranked list of files that are most likely to contain fuzz-worthy logic: parsing, decoding, deserialization, validation, protocol handling, file/network boundaries, or native bindings.\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Build a repository map\n    - Start with a repository overview using `tree` to identify:\n        - Root packages and layouts (src/ layout vs flat layout)\n        - Test directories and configs\n        - Bindings/native directories\n        - Examples, docs, and tooling directories\n    - Identify packaging and metadata files such as:\n        - pyproject.toml\n        - setup.cfg\n        - setup.py\n    - Identify test configuration and entry points:\n        - tests/\n        - conftest.py\n        - pytest.ini\n        - tox.ini\n        - noxfile.py\n\n2. Exclude low-value areas\n    - Skip: virtual environments, build outputs, vendored code, documentation-only directories, examples-only directories, generated files.\n\n3. Score files using explainable heuristics\n   Assign a file a higher score when it matches more of these indicators:\n    - Public API exposure: **init**.py re-exports, **all**, api modules\n    - Input boundary keywords in file path or symbols: parse, load, dump, decode, encode, deserialize, serialize, validate, normalize, schema, protocol, message\n    - Format handlers: json, yaml, xml, csv, toml, protobuf, msgpack, pickle\n    - Regex-heavy or templating-heavy code\n    - Native boundaries: ctypes, cffi, cython, extension modules, bindings\n    - Central modules: high import fan-in across the package\n    - Test adjacency: directly imported or heavily referenced by tests\n\n4. Rank and select\n    - Produce a Top-N list (default 10–30) with a short rationale per file.\n\n## Output format\n\nFor each file:\n\n- path\n- score (relative, not necessarily normalized)\n- rationale (2–5 bullets)\n- indicators_hit (list)\n\n---\n\n# Localize important functions\n\n## Goal\n\nFrom the important files, identify and rank functions or methods that are strong fuzz targets while minimizing full-body reading until needed.\n\n## Approach 1: AST-based header and docstring scan\n\nFor each localized Python file, parse it using Python’s `ast` module and extract only:\n\n- Module docstring\n- Function and async function headers (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class headers and method headers\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nDo not read full function bodies during the initial pass unless needed for disambiguation or final selection.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Build per-file declarations via AST\n    - Parse the file with `ast`\n    - Enumerate:\n        - Top-level functions\n        - Classes and their methods\n        - Nested functions only if they are likely to be directly fuzzable via an exposed wrapper\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Signature details (as available from AST)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Location information (file, line range if available)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using headers and docstrings\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Use tests to refine candidate selection early\n    - Before reading full bodies, check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n\n4. Confirm with targeted reading only for top candidates\n   For the top candidates (typically 10–20), read the full function bodies and capture:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions\n    - Internal helpers called\n    - Error handling style and exception types\n    - Any obvious invariants and postconditions\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from targeted reading only)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n\n## Approach 2: Scanning all important files yourself\n\nFor each localized Python file, read the file contents directly to extract:\n\n- Module docstring and overall structure\n- Function and async function definitions (name, args, defaults, annotations, decorators)\n- Class definitions and their methods\n- Full function bodies and implementation details\n- Docstrings for modules, classes, and functions/methods\n\nThis approach reads complete file contents, allowing for deeper analysis at the cost of higher token usage.\n\n### Procedure\n\n1. Read important files sequentially\n    - For each file from the important files list, read the full contents.\n    - Extract by direct inspection:\n        - Top-level functions and their complete implementations\n        - Classes and their methods with full bodies\n        - Nested functions if they are exposed or called by public APIs\n    - For each symbol, collect:\n        - Fully-qualified name\n        - Complete signature (from source text)\n        - Decorators\n        - Docstring (if present)\n        - Full function body\n        - Location information (file, approximate line range)\n\n2. Generate an initial candidate set using full source analysis\n   Prioritize functions/methods that:\n    - Accept bytes, str, file-like objects, dicts, or user-controlled payloads\n    - Convert between representations (raw ↔ structured)\n    - Perform validation, normalization, parsing, decoding, deserialization\n    - Touch filesystem/network/protocol boundaries\n    - Call into native extensions or bindings\n    - Contain complex control flow, loops, or recursive calls\n    - Handle exceptions or edge cases\n    - Clearly document strictness, schemas, formats, or error conditions\n\n3. Analyze implementation details from full bodies\n   For each candidate function, inspect the body for:\n    - Preconditions and assumptions (explicit checks, assertions, early returns)\n    - Internal helpers called and their purposes\n    - Error handling style and exception types raised\n    - Invariants and postconditions (explicit or implicit)\n    - Statefulness and global dependencies\n    - Input transformations and data flow\n    - Native calls or external process invocations\n    - Resource allocation and cleanup patterns\n\n4. Use tests to refine candidate selection\n    - Check if tests reference these functions/modules:\n        - Direct imports in tests\n        - Fixtures that exercise particular entry points\n        - Parameterizations over formats and inputs\n    - Down-rank candidates that are already well-covered unless they are high-risk boundaries (parsers/native bindings).\n    - Note which aspects of each function are tested vs untested.\n\n5. Rank and shortlist\n   Rank candidates using an explainable rubric:\n    - Input surface and reachability\n    - Boundary risk (parsing/decoding/native)\n    - Structural complexity (from full body analysis)\n    - Existing test coverage strength and breadth\n    - Ease of harnessing\n    - Observable implementation risks (unsafe operations, unchecked inputs, complex state)\n\n### Output format\n\nFor each function/method:\n\n- qualname\n- file\n- line_range (if available)\n- score (relative)\n- rationale (2–6 bullets)\n- dependencies (key helpers, modules, external state)\n- harnessability (low/medium/high)\n- implementation_notes (key observations from body analysis)\n\n---\n\n# Summarize existing unit tests\n\n## Goal\n\nSummarize what is already tested, infer test oracles, and identify gaps that fuzzing can complement.\n\n## Hard requirement\n\nAlways inspect and incorporate existing tests in the repository when:\n\n- Ranking functions\n- Selecting FUTs\n- Designing input models and oracles\n- Proposing seed corpus sources\n\n## Procedure\n\n1. Inventory tests\n    - Locate tests and their discovery configuration (pytest.ini, pyproject.toml, tox.ini, noxfile.py).\n    - Enumerate test modules and map them to source modules via imports.\n    - Identify shared fixtures and data factories (conftest.py, fixture files, test utilities).\n\n2. Summarize test intent\n   For each test module:\n    - What behaviors are asserted\n    - What inputs are used\n    - What exceptions are expected\n    - What invariants are implied\n\n3. Infer oracles and properties\n   Common fuzz-friendly oracles include:\n    - Round-trip properties\n    - Idempotence of normalization\n    - Parser consistency across equivalent inputs\n    - Deterministic output given deterministic input\n    - No-crash and no-hang for malformed inputs\n\n4. Identify coverage gaps\n    - FUT candidates with no direct tests\n    - Input classes not covered by tests (size extremes, malformed encodings, deep nesting, edge unicode, invalid schemas)\n    - Code paths guarded by complex conditionals or exception handlers with no tests\n\n## Output format\n\n- test_map: module_under_test → tests → asserted behaviors\n- inferred_oracles: list of reusable invariants with the functions they apply to\n- gaps: ranked list of untested or weakly-tested candidates\n\n---\n\n# Decide function under test for fuzzing\n\n## Goal\n\nSelect a final set of FUTs (typically 1–5) and produce a structured \"note to self\" for each FUT so the agent can proceed to harness implementation, corpus seeding, and fuzz execution.\n\n## Selection criteria\n\nPrefer FUTs that maximize:\n\n- Security/robustness payoff (parsing, decoding, validation, native boundary)\n- Reachability with minimal setup\n- Low existing test assurance or narrow test input coverage\n- High fuzzability (simple input channel, clear oracle or crash-only target)\n\nExplicitly weigh:\n\n- What tests already cover (and what they do not)\n- What seeds can be extracted from tests, fixtures, and sample data\n\n## Required \"note to self\" template\n\nFor each selected FUT, produce exactly this structure:\n\nFuzzing Target Note\n\n- Target:\n- File / location:\n- Why this target:\n    - Input surface:\n    - Boundary or native considerations:\n    - Complexity or path depth:\n    - Current test gaps:\n- Callable contract:\n    - Required imports or initialization:\n    - Preconditions:\n    - Determinism concerns:\n    - External dependencies:\n- Input model:\n    - Primary payload type:\n    - Decoding or parsing steps:\n    - Constraints to respect:\n    - Edge classes to emphasize:\n- Oracles:\n    - Must-hold properties:\n    - Acceptable exceptions:\n    - Suspicious exceptions:\n    - Crash-only vs correctness-checking:\n- Harness plan:\n    - Recommended approach:\n    - Minimal harness signature:\n    - Seed corpus ideas:\n    - Timeouts and resource limits:\n- Risk flags:\n    - Native extension involved:\n    - Potential DoS paths:\n    - External I/O:\n- Next actions:\n    1.\n    2.\n    3.\n    4.\n\n## Output format\n\n- selected_futs: list of chosen FUTs with brief justification\n- notes_to_self: one \"Fuzzing Target Note\" per FUT\n\n---\n\n# Final JSON block\n\nAt the end of the report, include a JSON object with:\n\n- important_files: [{path, score, rationale, indicators_hit}]\n- important_functions: [{qualname, file, line_range, score, rationale, dependencies, harnessability}]\n- test_summary: {test_map, inferred_oracles, gaps}\n- selected_futs: [{qualname, file, line_range, justification}]\n- notes_to_self: [{target_qualname, note}]\n\n\nARGUMENTS: /app/black"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c4870ea9-83dc-4fcc-99e6-806ec7aea3b8","uuid":"9661fbf0-111c-45b2-bbd5-e96499c16ef1","isSynthetic":true}

As a result, this library\n    25→> has been put into a *maintenance-only* mode. Support for new Python versions\n    26→> will be added and critical bugs and security issues will still be\n    27→> fixed but all other changes will be rejected. Users are encouraged to migrate\n    28→> to [orjson](https://pypi.org/project/orjson/) which is both much faster and\n    29→> less likely to introduce a surprise buffer overflow vulnerability in the\n    30→> future.\n    31→\n    32→## Usage\n    33→\n    34→May be used as a drop in replacement for most other JSON parsers for Python:\n    35→\n    36→```pycon\n    37→>>> import ujson\n    38→>>> ujson.dumps([{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, True])\n    39→'[{\"key\":\"value\"},81,true]'\n    40→>>> ujson.loads(\"\"\"[{\"key\": \"value\"}, 81, true]\"\"\")\n    41→[{'key': 'value'}, 81, True]\n    42→```\n    43→\n    44→### Encoder options\n    45→\n    46→#### encode_html_chars\n    47→\n    48→Used to enable special encoding of \"unsafe\" HTML characters into safer Unicode\n    49→sequences. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /logs/agent/sessions/skills/fuzzing-python\n\n# Python Fuzzing Skill\n\n## Setting up fuzzing for a Python project\n\nFuzz testing for Python projects are Atheris.\nAtheris is a coverage-guided Python fuzzing engine.\nIt supports fuzzing of Python code, but also native extensions written for CPython.\nAtheris is based off of libFuzzer.\nWhen fuzzing native code,\nAtheris can be used in combination with Address Sanitizer or Undefined Behavior Sanitizer to catch extra bugs.\n\nYou can install prebuilt versions of Atheris with pip:\n\n```sh\npip3 install atheris\n```\n\nThese wheels come with a built-in libFuzzer,\nwhich is fine for fuzzing Python code.\nIf you plan to fuzz native extensions,\nyou may need to build from source to ensure the libFuzzer version in Atheris matches your Clang version.\n\n## Using Atheris\n\n### Example\n\n```python\n#!/usr/bin/python3\n\nimport atheris\n\nwith atheris.instrument_imports():\n  import some_library\n  import sys\n\ndef TestOneInput(data):\n  some_library.parse(data)\n\natheris.Setup(sys.argv, TestOneInput)\natheris.Fuzz()\n```\n\nWhen fuzzing Python, Atheris will report a failure if the Python code under test throws an uncaught exception.\n\n### Python coverage\n\nAtheris collects Python coverage information by instrumenting bytecode.\nThere are 3 options for adding this instrumentation to the bytecode:\n\n- You can instrument the libraries you import:\n\n  ```python\n  with atheris.instrument_imports():\n    import foo\n    from bar import baz\n  ```\n\n  This will cause instrumentation to be added to `foo` and `bar`, as well as\n  any libraries they import.\n\n- Or, you can instrument individual functions:\n\n  ```python\n  @atheris.instrument_func\n  def my_function(foo, bar):\n    print(\"instrumented\")\n  ```\n\n- Or finally, you can instrument everything:\n\n  ```python\n  atheris.instrument_all()\n  ```\n\n  Put this right before `atheris.Setup()`. This will find every Python function\n  currently loaded in the interpreter, and instrument it.\n  This might take a while.\n\nAtheris can additionally instrument regular expression checks, e.g. `re.search`.\nTo enable this feature, you will need to add:\n`atheris.enabled_hooks.add(\"RegEx\")`\nTo your script before your code calls `re.compile`.\nInternally this will import the `re` module and instrument the necessary functions.\nThis is currently an experimental feature.\n\nSimilarly, Atheris can instrument str methods; currently only `str.startswith`\nand `str.endswith` are supported. To enable this feature, add\n`atheris.enabled_hooks.add(\"str\")`. This is currently an experimental feature.\n\n#### Why am I getting \"No interesting inputs were found\"?\n\nYou might see this error:\n\n```\nERROR: no interesting inputs were found. Is the code instrumented for coverage? Exiting.\n```\n\nYou'll get this error if the first 2 calls to `TestOneInput` didn't produce any\ncoverage events. Even if you have instrumented some Python code,\nthis can happen if the instrumentation isn't reached in those first 2 calls.\n(For example, because you have a nontrivial `TestOneInput`). You can resolve\nthis by adding an `atheris.instrument_func` decorator to `TestOneInput`,\nusing `atheris.instrument_all()`, or moving your `TestOneInput` function into an\ninstrumented module.\n\n### Visualizing Python code coverage\n\nExamining which lines are executed is helpful for understanding the\neffectiveness of your fuzzer. Atheris is compatible with\n[`coverage.py`](https://coverage.readthedocs.io/): you can run your fuzzer using\nthe `coverage.py` module as you would for any other Python program. Here's an\nexample:\n\n```bash\npython3 -m coverage run your_fuzzer.py -atheris_runs=10000  # Times to run\npython3 -m coverage html\n(cd htmlcov && python3 -m http.server 8000)\n```\n\nCoverage reports are only generated when your fuzzer exits gracefully. This\nhappens if:\n\n- you specify `-atheris_runs=<number>`, and that many runs have elapsed.\n- your fuzzer exits by Python exception.\n- your fuzzer exits by `sys.exit()`.\n\nNo coverage report will be generated if your fuzzer exits due to a\ncrash in native code, or due to libFuzzer's `-runs` flag (use `-atheris_runs`).\nIf your fuzzer exits via other methods, such as SIGINT (Ctrl+C), Atheris will\nattempt to generate a report but may be unable to (depending on your code).\nFor consistent reports, we recommend always using\n`-atheris_runs=<number>`.\n\nIf you'd like to examine coverage when running with your corpus, you can do\nthat with the following command:\n\n```\npython3 -m coverage run your_fuzzer.py corpus_dir/* -atheris_runs=$(( 1 + $(ls corpus_dir | wc -l) ))\n```\n\nThis will cause Atheris to run on each file in `<corpus-dir>`, then exit.\nNote: atheris use empty data set as the first input even if there is no empty file in `<corpus_dir>`.\nImportantly, if you leave off the `-atheris_runs=$(ls corpus_dir | wc -l)`, no\ncoverage report will be generated.\n\nUsing coverage.py will significantly slow down your fuzzer, so only use it for\nvisualizing coverage; don't use it all the time.\n\n### Fuzzing Native Extensions\n\nIn order for fuzzing native extensions to be effective, your native extensions\nmust be instrumented. See [Native Extension Fuzzing](https://github.com/google/atheris/blob/master/native_extension_fuzzing.md)\nfor instructions.\n\n### Structure-aware Fuzzing\n\nAtheris is based on a coverage-guided mutation-based fuzzer (LibFuzzer). This\nhas the advantage of not requiring any grammar definition for generating inputs,\nmaking its setup easier. The disadvantage is that it will be harder for the\nfuzzer to generate inputs for code that parses complex data types. Often the\ninputs will be rejected early, resulting in low coverage.\n\nAtheris supports custom mutators\n[(as offered by LibFuzzer)](https://github.com/google/fuzzing/blob/master/docs/structure-aware-fuzzing.md)\nto produce grammar-aware inputs.\n\nExample (Atheris-equivalent of the\n[example in the LibFuzzer docs](https://github.com/google/fuzzing/blob/master/docs/structure-aware-fuzzing.md#example-compression)):\n\n```python\n@atheris.instrument_func\ndef TestOneInput(data):\n  try:\n    decompressed = zlib.decompress(data)\n  except zlib.error:\n    return\n\n  if len(decompressed) < 2:\n    return\n\n  try:\n    if decompressed.decode() == 'FU':\n      raise RuntimeError('Boom')\n  except UnicodeDecodeError:\n    pass\n```\n\nTo reach the `RuntimeError` crash, the fuzzer needs to be able to produce inputs\nthat are valid compressed data and satisfy the checks after decompression.\nIt is very unlikely that Atheris will be able to produce such inputs: mutations\non the input data will most probably result in invalid data that will fail at\ndecompression-time.\n\nTo overcome this issue, you can define a custom mutator function (equivalent to\n`LLVMFuzzerCustomMutator`).\nThis example produces valid compressed data. To enable Atheris to make use of\nit, pass the custom mutator function to the invocation of `atheris.Setup`.\n\n```python\ndef CustomMutator(data, max_size, seed):\n  try:\n    decompressed = zlib.decompress(data)\n  except zlib.error:\n    decompressed = b'Hi'\n  else:\n    decompressed = atheris.Mutate(decompressed, len(decompressed))\n  return zlib.compress(decompressed)\n\natheris.Setup(sys.argv, TestOneInput, custom_mutator=CustomMutator)\natheris.Fuzz()\n```\n\nAs seen in the example, the custom mutator may request Atheris to mutate data\nusing `atheris.Mutate()` (this is equivalent to `LLVMFuzzerMutate`).\n\nYou can experiment with [custom_mutator_example.py](example_fuzzers/custom_mutator_example.py)\nand see that without the mutator Atheris would not be able to find the crash,\nwhile with the mutator this is achieved in a matter of seconds.\n\n```shell\n$ python3 example_fuzzers/custom_mutator_example.py --no_mutator\n[...]\n#2      INITED cov: 2 ft: 2 corp: 1/1b exec/s: 0 rss: 37Mb\n#524288 pulse  cov: 2 ft: 2 corp: 1/1b lim: 4096 exec/s: 262144 rss: 37Mb\n#1048576        pulse  cov: 2 ft: 2 corp: 1/1b lim: 4096 exec/s: 349525 rss: 37Mb\n#2097152        pulse  cov: 2 ft: 2 corp: 1/1b lim: 4096 exec/s: 299593 rss: 37Mb\n#4194304        pulse  cov: 2 ft: 2 corp: 1/1b lim: 4096 exec/s: 279620 rss: 37Mb\n[...]\n\n$ python3 example_fuzzers/custom_mutator_example.py\n[...]\nINFO: found LLVMFuzzerCustomMutator (0x7f9c989fb0d0). Disabling -len_control by default.\n[...]\n#2      INITED cov: 2 ft: 2 corp: 1/1b exec/s: 0 rss: 37Mb\n#3      NEW    cov: 4 ft: 4 corp: 2/11b lim: 4096 exec/s: 0 rss: 37Mb L: 10/10 MS: 1 Custom-\n#12     NEW    cov: 5 ft: 5 corp: 3/21b lim: 4096 exec/s: 0 rss: 37Mb L: 10/10 MS: 7 Custom-CrossOver-Custom-CrossOver-Custom-ChangeBit-Custom-\n === Uncaught Python exception: ===\nRuntimeError: Boom\nTraceback (most recent call last):\n  File \"example_fuzzers/custom_mutator_example.py\", line 62, in TestOneInput\n    raise RuntimeError('Boom')\n[...]\n```\n\nCustom crossover functions (equivalent to `LLVMFuzzerCustomCrossOver`) are also\nsupported. You can pass the custom crossover function to the invocation of\n`atheris.Setup`. See its usage in [custom_crossover_fuzz_test.py](src/custom_crossover_fuzz_test.py).\n\n#### Structure-aware Fuzzing with Protocol Buffers\n\n[libprotobuf-mutator](https://github.com/google/libprotobuf-mutator) has\nbindings to use it together with Atheris to perform structure-aware fuzzing\nusing protocol buffers.\n\nSee the documentation for\n[atheris_libprotobuf_mutator](contrib/libprotobuf_mutator/README.md).\n\n## Integration with OSS-Fuzz\n\nAtheris is fully supported by [OSS-Fuzz](https://github.com/google/oss-fuzz), Google's continuous fuzzing service for open source projects. For integrating with OSS-Fuzz, please see [https://google.github.io/oss-fuzz/getting-started/new-project-guide/python-lang](https://google.github.io/oss-fuzz/getting-started/new-project-guide/python-lang).\n\n## API\n\nThe `atheris` module provides three key functions: `instrument_imports()`, `Setup()` and `Fuzz()`.\n\nIn your source file, import all libraries you wish to fuzz inside a `with atheris.instrument_imports():`-block, like this:\n\n```python\n# library_a will not get instrumented\nimport library_a\n\nwith atheris.instrument_imports():\n    # library_b will get instrumented\n    import library_b\n```\n\nGenerally, it's best to import `atheris` first and then import all other libraries inside of a `with atheris.instrument_imports()` block.\n\nNext, define a fuzzer entry point function and pass it to `atheris.Setup()` along with the fuzzer's arguments (typically `sys.argv`). Finally, call `atheris.Fuzz()` to start fuzzing. You must call `atheris.Setup()` before `atheris.Fuzz()`.\n\n#### `instrument_imports(include=[], exclude=[])`\n\n- `include`: A list of fully-qualified module names that shall be instrumented.\n- `exclude`: A list of fully-qualified module names that shall NOT be instrumented.\n\nThis should be used together with a `with`-statement. All modules imported in\nsaid statement will be instrumented. However, because Python imports all modules\nonly once, this cannot be used to instrument any previously imported module,\nincluding modules required by Atheris. To add coverage to those modules, use\n`instrument_all()` instead.\n\nA full list of unsupported modules can be retrieved as follows:\n\n```python\nimport sys\nimport atheris\nprint(sys.modules.keys())\n```\n\n#### `instrument_func(func)`\n\n- `func`: The function to instrument.\n\nThis will instrument the specified Python function and then return `func`. This\nis typically used as a decorator, but can be used to instrument individual\nfunctions too. Note that the `func` is instrumented in-place, so this will\naffect all call points of the function.\n\nThis cannot be called on a bound method - call it on the unbound version.\n\n#### `instrument_all()`\n\nThis will scan over all objects in the interpreter and call `instrument_func` on\nevery Python function. This works even on core Python interpreter functions,\nsomething which `instrument_imports` cannot do.\n\nThis function is experimental.\n\n#### `Setup(args, test_one_input, internal_libfuzzer=None)`\n\n- `args`: A list of strings: the process arguments to pass to the fuzzer, typically `sys.argv`. This argument list may be modified in-place, to remove arguments consumed by the fuzzer.\n  See [the LibFuzzer docs](https://llvm.org/docs/LibFuzzer.html#options) for a list of such options.\n- `test_one_input`: your fuzzer's entry point. Must take a single `bytes` argument. This will be repeatedly invoked with a single bytes container.\n- `internal_libfuzzer`: Indicates whether libfuzzer will be provided by atheris or by an external library (see [native_extension_fuzzing.md](./native_extension_fuzzing.md)). If unspecified, Atheris will determine this\n  automatically. If fuzzing pure Python, leave this as `True`.\n\n#### `Fuzz()`\n\nThis starts the fuzzer. You must have called `Setup()` before calling this function. This function does not return.\n\nIn many cases `Setup()` and `Fuzz()` could be combined into a single function, but they are\nseparated because you may want the fuzzer to consume the command-line arguments it handles\nbefore passing any remaining arguments to another setup function.\n\n#### `FuzzedDataProvider`\n\nOften, a `bytes` object is not convenient input to your code being fuzzed. Similar to libFuzzer, we provide a FuzzedDataProvider to translate these bytes into other input forms.\n\nYou can construct the FuzzedDataProvider with:\n\n```python\nfdp = atheris.FuzzedDataProvider(input_bytes)\n```\n\nThe FuzzedDataProvider then supports the following functions:\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeBytes(count: int)\n```\n\nConsume `count` bytes.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeUnicode(count: int)\n```\n\nConsume unicode characters. Might contain surrogate pair characters, which according to the specification are invalid in this situation. However, many core software tools (e.g. Windows file paths) support them, so other software often needs to too.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeUnicodeNoSurrogates(count: int)\n```\n\nConsume unicode characters, but never generate surrogate pair characters.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeString(count: int)\n```\n\nAlias for `ConsumeBytes` in Python 2, or `ConsumeUnicode` in Python 3.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeInt(int: bytes)\n```\n\nConsume a signed integer of the specified size (when written in two's complement notation).\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeUInt(int: bytes)\n```\n\nConsume an unsigned integer of the specified size.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeIntInRange(min: int, max: int)\n```\n\nConsume an integer in the range [`min`, `max`].\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeIntList(count: int, bytes: int)\n```\n\nConsume a list of `count` integers of `size` bytes.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeIntListInRange(count: int, min: int, max: int)\n```\n\nConsume a list of `count` integers in the range [`min`, `max`].\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeFloat()\n```\n\nConsume an arbitrary floating-point value. Might produce weird values like `NaN` and `Inf`.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeRegularFloat()\n```\n\nConsume an arbitrary numeric floating-point value; never produces a special type like `NaN` or `Inf`.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeProbability()\n```\n\nConsume a floating-point value in the range [0, 1].\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeFloatInRange(min: float, max: float)\n```\n\nConsume a floating-point value in the range [`min`, `max`].\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeFloatList(count: int)\n```\n\nConsume a list of `count` arbitrary floating-point values. Might produce weird values like `NaN` and `Inf`.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeRegularFloatList(count: int)\n```\n\nConsume a list of `count` arbitrary numeric floating-point values; never produces special types like `NaN` or `Inf`.\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeProbabilityList(count: int)\n```\n\nConsume a list of `count` floats in the range [0, 1].\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeFloatListInRange(count: int, min: float, max: float)\n```\n\nConsume a list of `count` floats in the range [`min`, `max`]\n\n```python\ndef PickValueInList(l: list)\n```\n\nGiven a list, pick a random value\n\n```python\ndef ConsumeBool()\n```\n\nConsume either `True` or `False`.\n\n## Important considerations for fuzz targets\n\nSome important things to remember about fuzz targets:\n\n- The fuzzing engine will execute the fuzz target many times with different inputs in the same process.\n- It must tolerate any kind of input (empty, huge, malformed, etc).\n- It must not exit() on any input.\n- It may use threads but ideally all threads should be joined at the end of the function.\n- It must be as deterministic as possible. Non-determinism (e.g. random decisions not based on the input bytes) will make fuzzing inefficient.\n- It must be fast. Try avoiding cubic or greater complexity, logging, or excessive memory consumption.\n- Ideally, it should not modify any global state (although that’s not strict).\n- Usually, the narrower the target the better. E.g. if your target can parse several data formats, split it into several targets, one per format.\n\n\nARGUMENTS: /app/arrow"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"c4870ea9-83dc-4fcc-99e6-806ec7aea3b8","uuid":"1e46252c-7adc-4ecf-b3ed-17139b76f0e8","isSynthetic":true}
